Homeless guy here, I literally sit in my tent and listen to these classes...going through the whole channel. I was in juvie when I was a kid and this channel is really explaining alot, helping me connect the dots.

Edit: Thank you for all the well wishes, most kind. Unfortunately things have taken a turn for the worse. I have gotten a place to stay and though I am not homeless anymore, I was attacked while walking to my tent after an argument with a drug user. He passed away shortly after the attack. So,out of the frying pan into the fire. I'm facing trial next month and am currently out on bail.

>
> My situation isn’t as bad as yours - but that’s not relevant & victimhood isn’t a competition - I just want to say that your comment moved me so much because I also face no housing, have been in temporary housing for years, I have quite serious & scary disabilities not being dealt with, not enough money, & no one in my life that cares.   
>   
> Understanding how society got like this helps. Because I don’t understand!   
>   
> These lectures are saving me somehow. I listen as I go to sleep & they’re on when I wake up. I feel safer.   
>   
> When I saw your comment my heart connected to your words & so knowing the pain of not knowing any safety up ahead in life, I know there are no words to make anything better. Well wishers surround me with words. But never help me in real life.  
>   
>  I spent my healthy life with homeless friends in my home, helping people not be forced to leave the country. Helping folks be safe. Get benefits. Everything. And I sometimes hate the world and everyone in it for none of that empathy & time being returned to me. When needed it. And what I’ve found is that occasionally just saying hi 👋🏻 to people online who also feel unsafe and have to force hope for the future into their minds to keep going another day, makes things better somehow. Not to be alone, in this lack of safety.   
>   
> Even if I’d spent my life not helping anyone else, I still would expect more assistance than I’ve received. I didn’t help others for payment in return one day. I don’t believe in that shit. But somehow that was where my brain went when the same people turned their backs on me.    
>   
> It was as if I understood the world and humans. And then suddenly I knew nothing at all! I was wrong I was an anomaly. I wasted my life when I should’ve been more selfish. I messed it all up with only myself to blame. I don’t know if that makes sense but I think a lot of people feel that their situation is all their fault whether they did things right or wrong in life. The end thoughts are the same. It’s messed up. It’s not our fault. We only play the cards we’re dealt.   
>   
> So I just wanted to say, you’re not alone, and hi 👋🏻, and I see you.   
>   
> And thank you professor Jiang for creating something that takes me away from daily life and able to understand the world around me - the change society is going through - i don’t know exactly why this helps more than anything else, but it does.   
>   
> So yeah… hello, and thank you for being vulnerable and honest. I wish everyone was able to be, but I understand why they can’t. And right now I feel lucky just to have a phone with internet and a roof over my head.   
>   
> Good luck fellow human. Because I don’t believe in god, & people let me down, but luck exists & if we interact with the right people at the right time, one housing officer, one advocate, one judge, everything can change. And get better. Keeping hope alive however we can.   
>   
> Thank you professor Jiang and your wonderful wife for these wonderful adventures into education!  

> **@Yeimi66**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=Ugx4PVkFiBx9JGe01zl4AaABAg.ARLmFtKc1DAAVE3eEGOkSA)  
>
> @mmn@mmnotes95 me it was an abusive family, so no one to support me, and then disability making me unable to socialise. And then nobody who I called friends came to visit me and I couldn’t go out. And that just spirals because the healthcare and housing systems are underfunded as hell. And certain types of disability are more ‘viable’, more important. For example if I was in a wheelchair instead of having seizures, I’d have help. If I was blind all the time instead of sometimes, I’d have access to charities for blindness. If I was going to die ie: terminal diagnosis, I’d get help to die ‘better’. If I attempted suicide of tried to kill someone and got a mental health diagnosis, I’d get help in one way or another. My mother was a drug addict with BPD so she got months in rehab and a home for the rest of her life at low rent. I’m physically unable to take of myself or do paperwork properly on time, so I fall through all the cracks. The system requires paperwork. Lots of it. Evidence. Hospital trips I can’t afford because I don’t have cab money or anyone to take me. How can I prove to benefits teams and housing officers that I can’t do paperwork without submitting paperwork? I can’t. And when this phone breaks, the basic one I will be able to afford may not even have disability settings and YouTube and I’m absolutely terrified.   
> I had a plan save an enough for a one way ticket to a desert island as I thought sea air & homelessness in warm weather might help. But I can’t afford the ticket.   
> That’s one way it happens.   
> And everyone I speak to on the phone whether medical or financial says “you must have someone who can help you?”  
> And I cry. Because if your own family doesn’t care or is abusive. You are out of luck.   
>   
> Also disability makes people want to look away. That’s why we have marches for gay pride, for Palestine, for everything, but who marches for disability except a few ‘healthy’ disabled people ie wheelchair users. And the family that takes care of them.   
>   
> I can’t march for myself. Disabled people make others afraid as they see themselves int hat situation and it’s ’scary and gross’. They find ways to blame the disabled person. They say “if you were a good human, you’d have people” or “I’d help you if I could” while going out for a nice dinner as a ‘treat’ while you have no food. They get scared you’ll get more sick and they’ll end up having to take care of you, wipe your ass, and that’s everyone’s worst nightmare. They feel helping you will make them more vulnerable financially and socially and they then have more chance to end up like you.   
>   
> There’s so many reasons. But mental or physical disability and/or abusive & neglectful families, are almost always the reasons involved.   
>   
> And we, as in the forgotten people, often commit suicide. So our stories are never heard. It’s the only way out. Read the other comments. Many people are kind but many also feel that prayers or talking to all the many good people int he world, or just being ‘better’ at life, will help.   
>   
> I spent my life working in care, I looked after homeless people in my own home for months, I volunteered my time and energy to help people get disability, I got people housed, I got them out of dangerous situations. That was my calling. So paid my taxes. I did it all ‘right’. And still end up like this. My only regret is not being more selfish. Not saving my time and energy for myself. Not saving money instead of helping others. I don’t regret how I lived my life for any reason except that if I could’ve seen my future, I could’ve made sure this didn’t happen. Been safer. So yeah maybe it was my fault in that way. I should’ve been more selfish and helped people less.   
>   
> Whatever we did in life, it’s always our own fault why we ended up like this. If we cling to money and prepare for bad time that may come, we were bad people and that’s why we have no people to help us now. If we do the opposite we were stupid to not prepare for the future better. We can’t win. It’s all my own fault. That’s what everyone around me who has control in one way or another thinks. I know that.   
>   
> “Anyone who can end up homeless obviously wasn’t a nice person cos that’d never happen to me” “or they were stupid and messed up their life”.   
>   
> Because everyone thinks it could never happen to them.   
>   
> Hope that helps you understand 🙏🏻 Bless you for asking, few people ever do. I hope life treats you well ❤   
>   
> Any other homeless people feel free to add your reasons…

> Parsis didn’t just “become” Zoroastrian. We’re the tail end of a line that starts before written history, runs through Zarathustra’s reform, survives the Achaemenids and Sasanians, gets nearly erased after the Arab conquest, and then gets carried across the sea by a handful of families who refused to let the fire go out.  
>   
> The Shahnameh doesn’t need to say “they are Zoroastrian” because the whole epic breathes the worldview we inherited. Asha, farr, truth against the lie, kings judged by righteousness, fire as witness. Ferdowsi doesn’t label it because in his world it’s the air everyone is already breathing.  
>   
> So yes, Parsis are Zoroastrian. Not by myth or nostalgia, but because we preserved the same ideas, prayers, and ethics from that ancient Indo-Iranian world to the Gujarat landing to the diaspora now. The line never snapped.

>   
> Apostle John → St. Polycarp of Smyrna (John’s disciple) Polycarp → St. Irenaeus of Lyon (his student) Irenaeus → Hippolytus of Rome (his student) and you can literally keep following that line to the bishops of today.  
>   
> These are real historical people, mentioned in Roman, Greek, and Syriac sources, whose writings still exist. Every Orthodox bishop today can trace his ordination lineage back through similar chains to the apostles. The Church is a physical, historical body, not an abstract idea.

>
> @off6848 I think we may be touching different layers of the same question.  
>   
> I’m not asserting that Truth is manufactured by man or that it progresses from primitive to advanced. If Truth is primordial and permeating, I’m not opposed to that framing.  
>   
> My point was simpler — that human beings encounter, interpret, and articulate what they perceive as truth within time. Even if Truth itself is timeless, our language, symbols, and structures are not. They arise in history because we arise in history.  
>   
> Recognizing that doesn’t confine me to a modern rationalist telos. It just acknowledges the medium through which finite minds engage what may be infinite.  
>   
> So I’m not reducing Truth to development — I’m distinguishing between Truth itself and our evolving participation in it.

An Iranian fan here. Thank you for presenting these ideas with such clarity. You should definitely look into The Book of Kings (Shahnameh) by the Persian poet Ferdowsi. One of the most powerful stories in the Shahnameh is the epic conflict between Rostam and Esfandiar. Beyond being a tragic hero’s tale, the story reflects a deeper tension between the older Iranian religious traditions (often linked to pre-Zoroastrian, possibly Mithraic/Indo-Iranian or mother-goddess–related beliefs) and the newer Zoroastrian order. Ferdowsi's work is not just a literary masterpiece — it is the backbone of the Persian language’s survival and many believe him to be the GOAT of Iranian poetry. Many Iranians see the Shahnameh a cultural anchor that has carried the language and its core beliefs through centuries. If you haven’t explored his work yet, I think you may find it a fascinating parallel to the themes you discuss.

>   
> "People have never asked me as they should have done, what the name of Zarathustra precisely meant in my mouth, in the mouth of the first immoralist; for that which distinguishes this Persian from all others in the past is the very fact that he was the exact reverse of an immoralist. Zarathustra was the first to see in the struggle between good and evil the essential wheel in the working of things. The translation of morality into the realm of metaphysics, as force, cause, end-in-itself, is his work. But the very question suggests its own answer. Zarathustra created this most portentous of all errors,—morality; therefore he must be the first to expose it. Not only because he has had longer and greater experience of the subject than any other thinker,—all history is indeed the experimental[Pg 134] refutation of the theory of the so-called moral order of things,—but because of the more important fact that Zarathustra was the most truthful of thinkers. In his teaching alone is truthfulness upheld as the highest virtue—that is to say, as the reverse of the cowardice of the "idealist" who takes to his heels at the sight of reality. Zarathustra has more pluck in his body than all other thinkers put together. To tell the truth and to aim straight: that is the first Persian virtue. Have I made myself clear? ... The overcoming of morality by itself, through truthfulness, the moralist's overcoming of himself in his opposite—in me—that is what the name Zarathustra means in my mouth."

As a Buddhist, I would like to make a little note. Buddhism is very diverse, so maybe there are sects like that, but for the sect I practice, it is not about rejecting anger hate, or any negative emotions, but meeting everything with love and cultivating a consciousness that is beyond the dualities. By doing so, we are able to fully experience the emotions while responding to them correctly with correct measures of virtue. It is not about embracing them or shoving them away, but allowing anything to come and realizing that the truth is beyond the appearances.

### **@elsawinston1562** | 👍 26 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=Ugyh-PWAqCT2mk2nRxR4AaABAg)

A story I'd share here is when I was in school, throughout the years, I never felt smart. Never felt like I couldnt quite understand what I was being taught, but mostly, didn't understand WHY I was being taught what I was. It was when I took a humanities course in high school that something finally clicked. It was a class that covered a lot of ground; studying religion, philosophy, various art movements, all on a very basic level. It was only in that class that I understood what I was learning and understood WHY I was being taught what I was. 

When I got to college, I was undecided for my first year. You have to take a class to try and decide your major, and when I saw my university had an art history degree I was like hell yeah!! Let's do that!! And I guess I'm bringing this up because my choosing of that major didn't come from a place of wanting to make money (lol), wanting to make a name for myself in any way, but because I knew in my soul that I could best attempt to understand the world through learning art history. I didn't have any other end goal then just to learn, because learning art history came very naturally to me. 

So when the professor speaks of finding "truth" in your life, and nourishing your own "truth", this is one of the first instances where I found it. I found what enriched my soul and found I had a desire to use art history as a tool to learn and teach, not only myself, but also the people around me. 

This lecture really, REALLY, spoke to me. Thank you professor!!

And I guess as a side note, I know art history isn't the end all and be all of understanding. But it is a great foundation for me to build my own understanding.

I'm a Christian and I consider Zoraster a prophet in the history of our tradition.

A concept I borrow from Islam's inclusion of them as "People of the Book" and the progressive revelation concept of the Baha'i.

My thinking is essentially this: the first mention of "The Kingdom of God" doesn't come from a Jewish or Christian source. It comes from the Gothas written by Zoraster himself. And when I read the Gothas as a Christian I realized he was talking about the same vision of a just world that Jesus of Nazareth was.

If God thought these people were backward Pagans then why did he entrust the Wise Men (Zoroastrians) with the mission to warn Mary and Joseph that they must travel to Egypt?

He did because these were holy men connected with God. And quite frankly any version of Christianity that insists that Jesus alone is the truth completely ignores his parable about the good Samaritan.

Ibn-Arabi was right when he said "There are as many paths to God as there are souls in the universe."

And if anyone insists that their version of Christianity is the one true way then they're full of shit.

>   
> The quote I used by Ibn-Arabi means what I claim it does. Let me try my best to explain his philosophy as I have come to understand it using direct quotes.  
>   
> I shared that quote as it was paraphrased into English when I first read it. But here's the actual words of Ibn-Arabi:  
>   
> الطرق إلى الله بعدد أنفاس الخلائق  
>   
> Which says literally:   
>   
> "The ways to God are as numerous as the breaths of creation."  
>   
> Every soul perceives God according to their own form. And therefore no two paths to God can be identical.  
>   
> Ibn-Arabi did believe that Islam was the path to God. But what did he mean by Islam?  
>   
> To him it was something more like all forms of the subordination of all creation to the Divine, rather than the particular institutionalized and legal tradition we call Islam.  
>   
> Here is the passage where says this:  
>   
>   
> مَا عَبَدَ أَحَدٌ مِنَ الْعَالَمِ غَيْرَ اللَّهِ، وَلَكِنِ اخْتَلَفَتِ الْوُجُوهُ فِي الْعِبَادَةِ. فَالْعَابِدُ إِنَّمَا يَعْبُدُ الْحَقَّ فِي كُلِّ مَعْبُودٍ، غَيْرَ أَنَّهُ يَغْلَطُ فِي التَّعْيِينِ. فَيَرَى الصُّورَةَ فَيَقِفُ عِنْدَهَا، وَلَوْ رَفَعَ الطَّبَعُ حِجَابَهُ لَعَلِمَ أَنَّهُ يَعْبُدُ اللَّهَ لَا غَيْرَهُ. فَمَا ثَمَّ إِلَّا اللَّهُ.  
>   
> Which says literally:  
>   
> No being in the world has ever worshipped anything other than God, but the forms of their worship differ. Thus the worshipper only ever worships the Real in every object of worship, though he errs in specifying the form. He sees the outward form and stops at it, but if the veil of his nature were lifted, he would know that he worships none but God. For there is nothing there but God.

>   
> B) Was a religion that geographically existed alongside Judaism and then Christianity so had intellectual exchange with them.   
>

>
> ​@AdilMinocherhomjee Glad to be talking with a modern Zorastrian 🔥☺️  
>   
> I think they're connected through the spirit of Asha and the vision of the Kingdom of God.  
>   
> Zoraster talks about the Kingdom of God in the Gathas, and it's these teachings that become the model of a just kingdom adopted by the Achaemenids and through them the world.  
>   
> Jesus Christ also talks about the Kingdom of God. Before Messiah which means annointed one became conflated with God, it was used to describe kings and prophets chosen by God.  
>   
> Well the book of Ezra is clear. Cyrus was one of these annointed kings chosen by God. That's why he was entrusted to help build the Second Temple where I believe God once dwelled.  
>   
> Jesus' message when you look at the quotes attributed to him alone is far more simple than the Christian theology that coalesced afterwards. It's Saint Paul and that faction of Christians that created the theology of attonement: the idea that God became a man to sacrifice himself to himself to forgive humanity of our sins - something God always had the ability to do.  
>   
> As Jiang put it in one of his lectures: "this doesn't make any sense."  
>   
> But it wasn't Paul that took over Jesus' ministry in Jerusalem after his death. It was Jesus' brother James the Just.  
>   
> The Book of James is the recording of his teachings. And in it he doesn't preach attonement or salvation from declaring faith in Jesus alone. He says that faith is nothing without works.  
>   
> This to me is the true spirit of the faith. The Kingdom of God Christ says is within us and among us.

> Just finished reading the Book of James (KJV), and it struck me how close James feels to the Gathas.  
>   
> James 5:19–20 says that if someone wanders from the truth and you bring them back, you’ve saved their life. Yasna 45.1 makes the same move: when someone slips into druj, the real service is helping their mind return to asha. Not through magic. Through clarity.  
>   
> James calls it saving a soul. Zarathustra frames it as restoring right-seeing. Different language. Same pattern of responsibility.  
>   
> And that lines up with your point about the Kingdom of God being within us. In the Gathas, the “kingdom” is the world aligned with asha. In James, it’s the same inner alignment expressed as works. Both traditions collapse salvation down to how people actually live, choose, and correct one another.  
>   
> The split between James and Paul reminds me of another early fracture: Ali versus Abu Bakr. James and Ali represent continuity with the originating message lived as ethic; Paul and Abu Bakr represent the institutional turn, where theology or authority expands beyond the founder’s immediate circle.   
>   
> It’s the same pattern of tension: preservation of the inner path versus construction of an outer system.  
>   
> What we're seeing in James, Paul, Ali, and Abu Bakr is not four men in disagreement but the recurring hinge where a movement decides whether its kingdom is inward, outward, or both.

>   
> Asha being what is good, but also what is true, and what is well ordered.

> Ahura Mazda isn’t a sky-king who forces history toward the good. The name gives the real architecture away. Mazda is wisdom. Not moral sentiment. Not cosmic favoritism. Wisdom as the fundamental pattern that makes truth possible at all. That’s why Zoroastrianism uses fire as its symbol. Fire doesn’t comfort. Fire reveals. It refines what can withstand clarity and burns off what can’t. It is the closest physical metaphor for a universe where what is real survives illumination and what is false collapses under it.  
>   
> Asha sits inside that hierarchy. Not as “goodness,” but as the state of perceiving reality without distortion. It’s what the world looks like when wisdom actually lands in a mind. And this is the part that deepens your intuition: evil, in this system, can’t endure not because the good is stronger, but because confusion has no structural integrity. Misalignment always exhausts itself. Entropy devours what can’t cohere. Asha isn’t moral triumph. It’s coherence winning by default.  
>   
> And it’s wild how other mythmakers stumbled onto the same metaphysics. Lucas’s early drafts called the Light Side “Ashla,” echoing Asha almost syllable for syllable. Not as imitation but as instinct. A sense that the universe contains a luminous alignment you can join or resist, but never defeat. Ahura Mazda as wisdom. Fire as perception. Asha as the field where truth becomes action.  
>

The claim that “everyone will be forgiven, including Hitler” seems to many people:
	•	Morally outrageous
	•	A betrayal of victims
	•	A trivialization of evil
	•	Effectively permission for any behavior
But the lecturer is making a subtle argument that deserves careful consideration.
Forgiveness ≠ Approval
To say Hitler will be forgiven doesn’t mean:
	•	What he did was acceptable
	•	His victims don’t matter
	•	There’s no moral difference between Hitler and others
	•	Evil has no consequences
Rather, it means:
	•	Ultimate reality (God/Ahura Mazda) is compassion, not vengeance
	•	Even the most evil person contains a divine spark that cannot be destroyed
	•	Hell is educational, not punitive—Hitler will fully experience the suffering he caused
	•	Eventually, after perhaps eons of reincarnation and learning, even Hitler’s soul can achieve enlightenment.

Beautiful lecture, Professor Jiang — thank you.
One small philosophical note.

Nietzsche’s Zarathustra is actually the opposite of the historical Zarathustra of the Gathas.

The real Zarathustra teaches Asha (cosmic moral truth), conscience, humility, and moral responsibility.
Nietzsche’s Zarathustra rejects all of that and goes in the opposite direction — more like Raskolnikov in Dostoyevsky, who attempts to live “beyond good and evil.”

### **@JoJoRock197** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgwHwjuCWYos6Hj3v7R4AaABAg)

I read “Thus Spoke Zarathustra” (Nietzsche) from cover to cover about 7 years ago, and I understood very little at the time. I really appreciate this video, and I’m watching it for the second time. I’ll be sure to put this back on my list to re-read.

Nietzsche used to suffer from seizures, which would give him visions. Medical professionals speculate that seizures would result in the release of DMT from the pineal gland, thus giving the patient a “psychedelic” experience—near-death, post-death visions, or otherworldly visions that somehow appear to be radically connected to the truth of this world.

Other than Nietzsche, it is speculated that Julius Caesar, and Prophet Mohammad both experienced seizures (the word seizure may have been derived from Caesar). In the Hadith and Sira, when Mohammad “receives a message” from the Angel Gabriel (الوحي جبريل) he was described by those who observed him to have fallen on the floor and convulsed for a while. Once the convulsions are over, Prophet Mohammad would tell what he saw in his visions.

Nietzsche’s seizures became more extreme the older he got, and he spent the last decade of his life in a psychotic break, almost a vegetable and uncommunicative. His sister handled whatever estate and writings he produced.

Myself I was fortunate to teach kids from all over Europe. 
Personally I think your lectures should be shown and shared in classes around the world.
I’m now in my 60s and I can say from years spent in Hell, betrayal and my own downfalls.
So I try hard not to judge, my Son woke me up to my greatest mistake, and that was competition, first in sports and later in anything that was winnable.
It may have come from rejection or poor performance in the classroom as a child.
Letting others win and seeing their sense of achievement and how they react.
I found more enjoyment in watching that than winning.
This war, I’ve been telling it was coming for many years, in 2016 my kids started to call me doomsdad.
Anyone could know it was coming as it’s the end of Pisces and Pisces reflects the time of Christianity.
Once telling my theory to students, one girl brought me back a book from her grandfather, the last chapter read virtually what I had said.
I was amazed as I only had the Stars, time at Stonehenge, (when you could sleep up there) and history.
That book came from Malaysia, something that probably wouldn’t get published in the west.
The book identifies the negative players in this war. You know we are not really allowed to name the bad players, it can get you killed.
Anyway I just wanted to show some appreciation for your generosity and hard work. Thank you

### **@dainiksanghvi8032**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgwDzyymddbLe1QAVsp4AaABAg)

Asha also means hope in Hindi (or the Indian language as many would understand here) 

Prof. Thank you for introducing Zorastrianism to me in such a simple manner and as I understand this I could find multiple parallels between Zorastrianism and Jainism and Buddhism. 
I am a follower of Jainism and it has similar philosophy that the biggest goal of a human is to perfect his/her soul and reach Moksh (the Nirvana, the state of transcendence, a state through which our soul escapes the cycle of continuous birth and death hence ending all the suffering of the soul- sounds a lot like Monad) 
Another aspect I would like to highlight about Jainism is that it gives importance to three things through which soul can attain perfection and reach Moksh and that is through Right Knowledge, Right Character, and Right Philosophy - The Gyaan, Charitra, and Darshan as we call it. Which can only be attained when we go on the path of truth (satya), non-violence(ahimsa), non-hoarding (hoarding of wealth- aparigraha), non-stealing (asteya), and even celibacy (to control our mind and senses). 

Jainism also says that the more good you do, the better birth you get, the bad you do the bad it gets, dividing the births into 4 categories -Animals, Humans, Demi gods (the powerful ones), and of hellish beings. And it says that only the Humans have the power and ability to escape this cycle and achieve Moksha (the Monad) and end all the suffering. 
I agree that organized religions have ruined the spiritual side of it and same has happened with Jainism over the years, I hope more and more people move towards the spiritual side of religions. 

Thank you for giving us valuable insights and teaching us so many things. I would be happy to discuss more on Jainism if needed

His explanation of Plato’s cave, returning to the cave to help others—this is actually Mahayana Philosophy projected onto Plato. Plato’s point was specifically about what we would called today the simulation. Not saying the world is or is not a simulation, but just that our senses aren’t truth seeking, but generate reality from a more fundamental reality. What we experience are the shadows on the wall. Plato cave is just this realization, and not any moral imperative.

---

### **@Sillyyouhahaaaaaay** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgzGcJOJciUnuV2Nz6t4AaABAg)

17:00

Most people focus on the young men in the story of the cave, but there’s a detail that often gets overlooked—the dog.

In Qur’an 18:18, the dog is described as:

“…stretching its forelegs at the entrance…”

Its position is specific:
	•	At the threshold
	•	Not fully inside, not fully outside
	•	Still, present, and in a natural state

Meanwhile, the humans inside the cave are in a completely different condition:
	•	They are put to sleep
	•	Turned to preserve their bodies
	•	Removed from normal time and causality

So you have a clear contrast:
	•	The humans are under direct divine intervention, suspended from ordinary reality
	•	The dog remains in a familiar, instinctive state, grounded and unchanged

What’s interesting is what happens after they wake up:
	•	The humans are confused
	•	They question time
	•	They are forced to interpret their reality and make decisions

The dog, however, is not described with any of this.

From this, a reflection emerges:

The humans represent beings with awareness, responsibility, and the burden of interpretation
While the dog represents existence without that burden—stable, instinctive, and untroubled

So the point isn’t that the dog is “better” or “freer,”
but that it highlights a distinction:

Between conscious existence that carries responsibility,
and natural existence that does not

And that contrast is placed exactly at the entrance of the cave—the boundary between two different states of being.

### **@omarshaaban907** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgyV9yRS32zSmF2li6t4AaABAg)

From what I understand, the true Zoroastrian view doesn’t erase judgment like the professor suggests . Souls DO pass through the Chinvat Bridge, and those who lived in alignment with the Lie enter a state that’s harsh and purifying. It’s not Eternal, but it is real. Asha restores the universe, but it doesn’t pretend that cruelty leaves no scar. So a Stalin doesn’t get a get of jail free card.

Where I struggle is this: it’s easier to embrace universal restoration when your children, your home, your life haven’t been touched by the worst things humans do - thankfully the professors children are not the victims of a gen0cide or trafficking so I can see why he opted for a simplistic explanation- he didn’t feel his words. 

When you’ve seen or imagined those extremes, the idea that every evil is eventually smoothed away feels too small for the weight of the world. 

I don’t oppose the hope in Asha, and I’m not disagreeing with the Professor for the sake of being a contrarian, but Dismissing hell entirely isn’t accurate. 


Zoroastrianism has judgment, has punishment, has a form of hell—just not an eternal one. Reducing the whole thing to ‘there’s no hell’ oversimplifies a tradition that takes wrongdoing very seriously.

i would like to add value of thought to this discussion. When Prof Jiang is talking about the unknown world and our world being a shadow world, Lets refer to video games as an example. The video game universe is only perceptible through a monitor where you can see the elements in 3D or 2D. When you unplug those outputs, it is basically non-existent and invisible. The controls and visual are what makes the game feel real. However, in reality, the video game universe at its core is a set of transistors, op amps and electronics. The 1st layer is electronics, the 2nd layer is the digital binary world, the 3rd layer is the computation world full of calculations, the 4th layer is the programming and logic world and the last layer is the perceptible world, the video game you see on the monitor. Hope this helps to expand your thinking. Just like in how our world is made up of vibrations, the video game world is made up of pixels. Our world is an illusion in a sense. What makes it real is our souls interacting with each other. The virtues and sins that we have is what makes this world creative and real to the Monad/God/Allah/Theeverlasting/The Source. You are right Mr Jiang, Everyone will be forgiven. In every monotheistic religion, god has always been described as the most forgiving or the one who constantly forgives. It is our sins and virtues that makes this world beautiful and creates a story out of nothingness. Nothingness is worse than virtue and sins which I am sure why the Monad craves. Sure the chaos may get out of control but it is the price to pay for creation. The imperfection is what creates something out of nothing.

> It only feels endless if you think Zoroastrianism is about reaching one perfect finish line.  
>

>   
> Every good choice makes the world a little better and that steady improvement is the whole point.  
>   
> Later stories by Zoroastrian priests in ~200 AD talked about a final ‘world renewal’ after being influenced by Abrahamic faiths but originally the renewal was something happening all the time.  
>   
> So originally Zoroastrianism isn’t being stuck…it’s simply a way of making life clearer and better, step by step.

It should also be noted that there were wide array of philosophical beliefs in the different forms of religions we cluster under the umbrella term of Zoroastrianism. The Persians were at the crossroads of the Indian, Babylonian, Greek and Semitic cultures.

### **@CharyG-d3z**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=Ugw9S4jpLR9D6QJFL5d4AaABAg)

Rumi was a devout, orthodox Muslim. He was not just a casual believer; he was a high-ranking Islamic scholar (faqih) who taught Sharia law in a madrassa.

Leading Rumi scholars (such as Franklin Lewis and the team at Dar-al-Masnavi) classify this "Only Breath" poem as apocryphal. It was likely written by a later poet or a folk mystic trying to sound like Rumi, or it is a "floating verse" that got attached to his name centuries later.

While Rumi did write about transcending labels. This is a core concept in Sufism (Islamic mysticism), but it is often precise theology that gets mistaken for secularism.

Rumi describes his own identity very clearly in an authentic quatrain (Rubaiyat):

    "I am the servant of the Quran as long as I have life. I am the dust on the path of Muhammad, the Chosen One. If anyone quotes anything except this from my talk, I am quit of him and outraged by these words."

The poems where he claims to have no religion are either mistranslations or poems written by someone else entirely.

As a Zoroastrian, a Persian, an Iranian, and an Aryan, I can assure you that you are not insulting or hurting Zoroastrian people by identifying as a Zoroastrian priest. By beginning your path, you have already reached this status.
Zarathustra is a principle; Zoroastrians are “cave-busters.” You are truly shattering the gates of hell. In German, the word for 'cave' (Höhle) is an anagram of the word for 'hell' (Hölle).
I am very happy to witness this process and would like to add something that might be helpful. The word for God—'God' (English), 'Gott' (German), 'Goda' (Sanskrit), or 'Khoda' (Persian)—essentially means 'yourself.'
To be convinced of God is to be convinced of yourself. However, if you “be-lie-ve” in yourself, you are living a lie with yourself.
The handshake with the right hand (the hand of truth) is an Avestan greeting ritual. When we greet someone, we say 'Truth be upon you' (Dorood bar shoma!). Even Namaste is an Avestan greeting ritual. This is why I believe that even Buddhism, Hinduism, Taoism, and Zen Buddhism all have their origins in Zoroastrianism.
I look forward to the moment you read the 'Shahnameh' (The Epic of Kings) by Ferdowsi. This spiritual warfare of the USA ('Med-usa') against Iran—the Land of Truth, Lightness, the Aryans, and the Noble—goes much deeper.
Perhaps you will find the time to read the books of Howdie Mickoski. I believe this is your ultimate journey; it marks the final stage of your journey.

30:44
Rumi was a Muslim, and the God he addresses in his poetry is Allah, not Ahura Mazda. I had never even considered the idea that his references to God could be connected to some other higher form, but based on what you explained, now I see that there can be an indirect connection.

There are two important points to keep in mind:

1. 	Understanding the historical connection between Islam and earlier Iranian religious traditions, with Zoroastrianism as their predecessor.

2. 	Recognizing that the Persian language Rumi uses, along with the broader Persian mystical imagination, carries pre‑Islamic traces that can relate to Zoroastrian concepts.

In this sense, there is a kind of cultural continuity where the Persian religious and philosophical heritage subtly shapes the perception of Allah, and Islam in the Persian world is often experienced through a lens formed by earlier Iranian thought, including Zoroastrianism.

### **@souljaaking94**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgznoO0Pv32POCsgldh4AaABAg)

Interestingly enough, Nietzsche chose Zarathustra as character to actually oppose all of his teachings. This was also one of the books in which we proclaimed "God is dead". Here a excerpt in which he explains why he chose him:

People have never asked me as they should have done, what the name of Zarathustra precisely meant in my mouth, in the mouth of the first immoralist; for that which distinguishes this Persian from all others in the past is the very fact that he was the exact reverse of an immoralist. Zarathustra was the first to see in the struggle between good and evil the essential wheel in the working of things. The translation of morality into the realm of metaphysics, as force, cause, end-in-itself, is his work. But the very question suggests its own answer. Zarathustra created this most portentous of all errors,—morality; therefore he must be the first to expose it. Not only because he has had longer and greater experience of the subject than any other thinker,—all history is indeed the experimental refutation of the theory of the so-called moral order of things,—but because of the more important fact that Zarathustra was the most truthful of thinkers. In his teaching alone is truthfulness upheld as the highest virtue—that is to say, as the reverse of the cowardice of the "idealist" who takes to his heels at the sight of reality. Zarathustra has more pluck in his body than all other thinkers put together. To tell the truth and to aim straight: that is the first Persian virtue. Have I made myself clear? ... The overcoming of morality by itself, through truthfulness, the moralist's overcoming of himself in his opposite—in me—that is what the name Zarathustra means in my mouth.

### **@teciardz** | 👍 1 likes

please allow me to drop my 2 cents here...

59:20 think the asha as an ocean, and we're just waves in it. we all are the ocean, but we as waves we are in states of being. the wave rises, reaches its peak, descends and reaches to the shore, and goes back to the ocean, back where it started.

another way of viewing it, it's as we are like soap bubbles. the visible part of the bubble, the outer sphere, is our body and our EGO. but inside, there's an air, that is a portion of all the air that is. while we are bubbles, we can say the air inside it are ours. but once it bursts, the outer sphere is gone, the air inside it merges with all the air that is. now you can't say what part of the air is you, or yours. you are all of it, or you can say you're none of it, you don't have any identity. your identity was the outer visible sphere, your body, or your EGO - your name, your nationality, your religion, your political position, your race, your gender, your social class, etc. so now that the bubble bursted, what are you? now, you come back to the source, or origins, and discover your TRUE nature - you go back to where you started.

another way of viewing it - thing of mathematical numbers:
the number zero is ALL and NOTHING at the same time. the zero is the starting point, the source and the center. from the zero, comes all of the other numbers; and because in this planet earth there is the polarity and duality, now there are positive and negative numbers. like this:

...-3 -2 -1 *0* +1 +2 +3...

when the numbers are contained in the zero, they're are not positive nor negative. they all ARE the zero - neutral. where they come from, and to where they're all going back eventually.
I mean, there are no two of the numbers. there's only one number, but in different states of being - negative or positive. this gives the illusion that there is two or multiple of them, but they are only one.
you can think of the zero as a mirror as well. when you see yourself in the mirror, there is not two of you, but only you and your image reflected and shifted in the mirror - everything is the same, but your right side is your left side in the mirror. your hands - they are equal, but one is right, other is left. your brain, there's the left side, and the right side, but really it's just one thing.

that's why right/wrong, good/bad, beautiful/ugly, left/right, black/white, male/female, love/hate, etc. are exactly ONE AND THE SAME, only in different states of being.
like a seesaw as well. the seesaw is one, but one side goes up while the other goes down simultaneously. they're not two distinct and separate things, they are only one thing, just shifting in between states of being. when both reaches the middle point, neither one can say it's either up or down.

just like a swing as well, you go on swinging, but when you stop playing it slowly stops and goes back to where it started - the center - not up, not down, not left not right.
like riding a bycicle as well - you can only move forward if you're able to balance in the middle. otherwise you're gonna fall either to the left or to the right. and that's part of learning to ride a bycicle lol. you need to make these mistakes in order to learn to ride a bicycle, and move forward.

we all ARE the asha, but life on earth and its densities and polarity aspects, makes us forget our true nature. while on earth we're just drunk, caught up in the net of illusions of polarity, identity and beliefs. the goal i guess is to get out of this net and "sober up", so we can remember our true nature, the asha, god, or whatever name you wanna call it.

I hope this helps. wishing you all the best!!

[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgxDBkUAxkQNOBaggTp4AaABAg)

I also want to address the way polytheistic systems were presented. Describing them primarily as systems where gods demand offerings or money in exchange for success in war feels like an oversimplification. Historically, many polytheistic traditions had complex theological, moral, and ritual frameworks, and reducing them to transactional practices risks misrepresenting them. 

Even if certain practices existed, presenting them as the defining feature creates a generalized and dismissive picture. A more precise approach would acknowledge both the diversity and the internal logic of those traditions, rather than reducing them to a single motive or behavior.

### **@michaels4255**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgxyML4NE1ys-iYdQXp4AaABAg)

"we don't know when he llived" - well, fifty years ago this was still debatable I think, but today the accumulated evidence points overwhelmingly to the 6th century BC, not to the Bronze Age. Sixth century in southeastern Iran or possibly Pakistan.

Also, some of Prof. Jiang's interpretations of Zoroastrianism sound suspiciously like the popular world view of young Baby Boomers in the 1960's, IOW I think there are some anachronistic projections in this lecture, although I feel I should include the caveat that I know a lot less about Zoroastrianism than I do about the 1960's. But I do know that in recent decades the consensus of historians who are interested in that period seems to be converging on the sixth century BC as the age of Zarathustra, not the second millennium BC, although those older dates are still often cited due to scholarly inertia. And ZoroastrianISM as we know it today does not unequivocally appear until the third century AD. (Yes, AD!)

I dont think Asha is different for everyone. Or at least if it is, I think it should also has a dimension where it is true or same for everyone. Like universal truths like mathematics or certain metaphysical ideas like Subject-Object Duality or Buddhist emptiness and relativity of creation (relation of being object to an absolute subject or awareness). In Buddhism they differentiate between two truths, relative and absolute. And Plato also differentiates between two or actually 4 levels of "truth" or episteme/knowledge/insight (I think). So it seems more reasonable to me to assume Asha refers to all dimensions of truths, lower and higher, relative and absolute, or just to the higher or absolute truth and that there is a universal virtue that is based on our mututal essence as or in consciousness. Thats where empathy derives from and why empathy and service are direct ways to enlightenment. Not by being themselves sufficient but because empathy and service ultimately quieten the Ego and superficial thought constructs to reveal the underlying essence. Therefore, service or love or the path of the heart leads one to deeper insights and self-realization.

### **@surbhi6448**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=Ugw19jVnDJ6GQU6crL54AaABAg)

Wanted to bring up a point and say that what we are learning about the Asha here and about this 'pursuit' of perfection, and how individuality is what matters; if we had to zoom out and think about it from the perspective of what kind of society or mindset that's creating in people such as when Prof was talking about Jews being very creative, then wouldn't this lend itself towards the mass individulism (selfishness, even if pushed to the extreme) and creativity? Even about the cave metaphor, it sounds like the kind of thought process that would create missionaries, for example.

I say this because I find myself disagreeing with a lot of what Nietzsche says. I could be wrong, of course, and this could be the actual 'correct' way to look at spirituality, but I think that you can't strip people down into just this 'core' of yourself. I don't think it exists. I think that we as people are collections of what we learn from each other. If we speak about it in terms of energy, then maybe we were just an energy, but our existence in the universe itself implies that we HAVE to interact with other energies. I don't think there is a 'pure form' to come to that leads towards Asha. I think Asha is more so in the community than anything, because I think if there is this pure forgiveness, pure love that the Asha represents, it comes in the form of the love that I offer to the people I love or the world, and the love that is echoed back to me from them

Professor, the original Persian version of the second poem by Rumi that you analyzed is as follows:

نه تـرسا و یهودیـم نه گبرم نه مسلمانم

نه شـرقیّم نه غـربیّم نه بـریّم نه بـحریّم

نه ارکـان طبـیعیّم نه از افـلاک گـردانم

نه از خاکم نه از بادم نه از آبم نه از آتش

نه از عرشم نه از فرشم نه از کونم نه از کانم

نه از هندم نه از چینم نه از بلغار و سقسینم

نه از مـلک عراقـینم نه از خـاک خراسانم

نه از دنیا نه از عقبی نه از جنت نه از دوزخ

نه از آدم نه از حوا نه از فـردوس رضـوانم

مـکانم لامـکان بـاشد نشانم بی نشـان باشد

نه تن باشد نه جان باشد که من از جان جانانم

دوئی از خود برون کردم یکی دیدم دو عالم را

یـکی جویم یکی کویم یکی دانم یکی خوانم

هو الاول هو الآخـر هو الظاهـر هو البـاطن

بـغیر از هو و یـا من هو دگر چیزی نمی دانم

زجان عشق سر مستم دو عالم رفت از دستم

بـجـز رندی و قـلاشی نبـاشد هیـچ سامانم

اگر در عمر خود روزی دمی بی او بر آوردم

از آن وقت و از آن ساعت زعمر خود پشیمانم

اگر دستم دهد روزی دمی با دوست در خلوت

دو عالـم زیـر پـا آرم دگـر دستـی بـرافشانم

عجب یاران چه مرغم من که اندر بیضه پرّانم

درون جـسم آب و گل همـه عشقم همـه جانم

الا ای شمس تبریزی چنان مستم در این عالم

کـه جـز مـستی و قـلاشی نبـاشد هیچ درمانم

### **@theoddone887**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgxkTS2wmd-9bXOzSlt4AaABAg)

1. The Shift to Monotheism & Dualism:



Ahura Mazda: The one supreme God, the "Lord of Wisdom," representing perfection, truth (Asha), and light (symbolized by fire).



Cosmic Dualism: The universe is a battleground between two fundamental, opposing forces:



Asha: Truth, order, righteousness, virtue, and creativity.



Druj: The Lie, chaos, deceit, and destruction.



Crucially, this battle is internal. It is fought within the human heart and mind, not just among gods. This internalizes morality.



2. The Three Revolutionary Pillars (The Categorical Imperative Anticipated):

You brilliantly connect these to Kant's later ideas:



The Primacy of the Individual: Salvation and judgment are personal. "What matters is what happens inside of us." The community, tribe, or nation does not save you; your individual choices do.



Free Will as Absolute: Virtue is only meaningful if chosen freely, without coercion. Even choosing evil is a necessary part of this free will, as struggle is essential for growth.



Human as an End, Not a Means: Every individual life is infinitely valuable and cannot be sacrificed for a supposed greater good. This condemns the brutality of war and empire.



3. The Path of Asha:



Asha is not just truth-telling, but active virtue expressed through Good Thoughts, Good Words, Good Deeds.



It is a responsibility. Like Plato's freed prisoner in the Allegory of the Cave, achieving enlightenment compels you to return and help others, despite the risk of rejection.



It is a process of becoming, not a final destination. It involves constant self-questioning, "burning yourself in your own flame" (Nietzsche), and reinvention.



4. Rejection of Corrupted Religion:



Zoroaster, as a priest, attacked the corrupt priesthood that exploited people.



You (and Nietzsche/Rumi) extend this to criticize organized religion that becomes a tool for control, in contrast to a personal, living faith focused on joy, creativity, and action.



True wisdom is found in the world and through personal struggle, not in the comfortable "dusty rooms" of scholarly institutions.



Historical and Philosophical Impact

Bridge to Major Religions: You correctly identify Zoroastrianism's profound influence on Judaism (during the Babylonian Exile), and subsequently on Christianity and Islam—concepts like monotheism, cosmic good vs. evil, final judgment, heaven/hell, and a savior figure (the Saoshyant).



Greek Philosophy: The influence on Plato (the Allegory of the Cave is a perfect parallel) and the pre-Socratics is noted, with Greeks considering Zoroaster a proto-philosopher and scientist.



Later Echoes: The lecture powerfully uses Rumi (for mystical unity and love) and Nietzsche's Thus Spoke Zarathustra (for the philosophy of self-overcoming, creativity, and embracing life's struggle) to show the timeless and adaptable nature of these core ideas.

### **@exxena**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgzXfJTN6_m807OcZkl4AaABAg)

Immanuel Kant wanted to formulate a thesis independent of religion that would be understandable to all people. Act according to the maxims (=highest personal rule of life) that you would like to see become universal laws. In other words, do not do to others, what you would not want them to do to you. According to Kant's school of legal philosophy, one is free as long as one does not restrict the freedom of others. In this case, one has created a maxim whereby others are also allowed to restrict one's freedom. Kant was not only concerned with the free will of the individual, but also with a religiously independent social contract in which everyone can exercise their free will without restricting that of a third party. Unfortunately, this does not work in a religious society, because here it is always about the will of God, which is proclaimed by a representative sent by God (prophet, saint, monarch, politicians, religious leaders). These interpretations of Persian texts are a modern rendition and not a reflection of ancient Persian times. This, in turn, distorts the prevailing image of that era.
It is unfortunate that you do not mention the Hittite Empire. The Hittites were a highly developed society with rules. And one of the most sacred and highest rules in the Hittite Empire was as follows:
                                                                 " Do not play with someone else's bread. "
This sacred cultural law, originally carved in stone by the Hittites, still applies in Anatolia and the Black Sea region. In ancient societies, the primary concern was securing one's existence. Only in such a social structure was it possible to develop culturally and as an individual. The most fascinating thing about Hittite culture was that they managed to establish peace in the Sumerian cities and much more. It is all the more unfortunate that you simply omit the most important Anatolian culture. The Hittites did not have a single ruler (King), but rather a ruling couple. They were equal. Man and woman ruled together. Men had only one wife. Marriages between siblings were forbidden. A woman's rank in society was determined by the number of children she had given birth to, among other things. The first peace treaty in history was between the Hittites and the Egyptians (among the Egyptians, marriage between siblings and with one's own nieces, daughters, and sisters was the norm, and a man had several wifes). I would be delighted if you would also devote a lecture to one of the most highly developed cultures of pre-antiquity, the Hittites.  Could you please explain how the Persian fire worshippers buried their dead? I think that says a lot about a culture. Thank you for your lectures, they make me think and I love that.

### **@morockapdx7174**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=Ugy2EShXEoz5WGt9Rn14AaABAg)

Two take aways. I may need to go back and see how this lecture series is framed, but it seems to present the theology as objective reality. Which makes this feel like a religious school. The way Nietzsche is presented is as, just another prophet of Zarathustra. Leaving out, that Nietzsche picked Zarathustra because he specifically blamed him for enslaving humanity to religiosity. And he attempted to recover spirituality both in the light of and in spite of, modernity. Thus Spoke Zarathustra next act is to declare, God is dead. Man, killed him. We live in the dead God’s shadow. 
My second takeaway is, I didn’t realize how much his philosophy, his new spiritualism parallels, Zoroastrianism. I technically knew this but hadn’t really put it together in its totality. But, how the embodied experience of reality, life and the power to life, is the replacement to a supernatural spiritualism. I think it is why there has never been an adequate word to replace spirit. 
What makes this profound to me, is it gives me a common ground with people who claim they feel something, and that is why that can’t completely abandon some form of spiritual thinking. The commonality is the embodied experience, the phenomenon. If it happens in your body, you feel it, than that is real. It maybe triggered differently, you may interpret it differently, but this is the common thread. An atheist, can experience profoundness, but does not attribute it to something supernatural. But lacks the language do describe if secularly. Well, except maybe of Nietzsche and a handful of other philosophers. 

This to me is the most interesting thing in the lecture. I can interpret the meaning and value of the lesson, but I will do so from a Nietzschean perspective. I don’t except the supernatural side of the narrative. Though, the return to the universalists form of religion is perhaps the most enlightened version. The one I can coexist best with.

Professor says this about Zarathustra's teaching of God, the Monad:  "The problem with perfection is that you lack imagination --  you lack creativity -- and that's why when God emanates, he created us, humans. Why? because we are material. We have bodies." This doesn't make any sense. Since God is perfect, He (or It) is completely imaginative and creative (because this is the meaning of perfection). God did not necessarily create humans with bodies -- a perfect God creates us as perfect spiritual beings, as He is, perfect beyond form. However, we think we are experiencing a fearful dream, an illusion in which parts of our Oneness as separated and limited to bodies -- this was not God's will -- and that is why we think we suffer in this world. We can experience remembering God and remembering who we are in truth by connecting with our Godly Source. Our own spiritual perfection will correct our mind's misperceptions if we are willing to be guided. We are already in the spirit world -- we never left. But in this world, we are using our minds to serve our perception of being separate images (minds and bodies). We need to use the world to wake up, not to call it "home" and try to fulfill ego goals for self-gratification. See the perfect spirit in everyone, as we were created. Realize that we are united as co-creator with our Source. Ask, listen, receive -- and give to others as ourselves.

"people [in ancient Persia] were in love with capitalism" - I know this word has always been poorly defined, but it is probably not accurate to speak of "capitalism" prior to the Industrial Revolution.  Capitalism is generally considered to be characterized by the dominance (not merely existence) of certain institutions such as free labor, capital markets, relatively low artificial barriers to trade, secure private property rights, a limited state, joint stock corporations with limited liability, and the availability of surplus energy resources, usually coal, oil, or gas, to fuel per capita economic growth.  (Coal use became common in England in the 16th century and grew exponentially for the next five centuries.)  These were not the dominant characteristics of pre - Modern economies.  Capitalism  is more than just trade or the profit motive.

Szeredásnak ő Ahura

Csütörtököt nem mond aura.







Önimádó bálvány vagyok,

Megtérek magamhoz, ragyog

Nyelvem arany nimbusza

Én istenkirály Baál himnusza





Valentin az én napom

Bálintra azt bólintom

Szerelmes az én papom

Baál int, ha akarom.





Látom az embereket, mint sok járó fát

Emberiségmassza hagyd el a járókát

Sártalan sarlatán sa-ta-ani síva-tag ciklus

Sok Szároszt túlélt sok mocsári ciprus



Csatahoni háborodott hébörgők

Kivágták a sok fát, s a mennydörgőt,

Baál istent bemocskolják

Kabbalának nevezik kék lovát.





A mágus isten magyar nyila telibe

Villámszerű csapás hozzon fényt a fejibe

Sok hitehagyott balga, Baálos

Belső napod újra tüzel pálos.





A mocsár latinóul pálus

Balázs nyelve magyar, mágus

Agyamban egység a három pólus

Mácsár nyelv fénye ingoványos





Milliárdok koronája az égből száll alá

Világ Kiss nagykirálya Baállá vált pala

Én napistim örök harmadik, szemes, utas.

Kiss Baáli vagyok, be nem nő a kutacs





Kiss Vuk vagyok, ma Tass, kutass

Nyelvi kútfő forrásában én vagyok a kutas

Aki a túlsó partra a szerelmet

Nyelvében tartva beszélget.



Lilliput legnagyobb királya

Óriás törpék törpe óriása

Javít tana, Nimród fia

Kám a bátya, s mit lát pina.





Jáfet, Tana

Nem árt tana

Nim ród, Anna

Nem halandzsa.

### **@Yllrro**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgxNLz423GeCoNfsddF4AaABAg)

You are missing so much of this puzzle Jiang. I don’t know where to begin, but the entire video is based on modern interpretations of what happened and not the actual fact and reality our common ancestors underwent in meeting with nature, existence and their own being. Mankind has been pursuing truth ever since he awoke to his own existence, which is what all our knowledge, religions, spiritualities and sciences are. They all came from the question: “who am I?” and it is this “I” our ancestors called God, creating the first idea of monotheism. However, this is in fact the birth of abstraction, as we have always viewed God as all, which is why we have interpreted our earlier ancestors as polytheistic which they weren’t. You have neglected that we as life, coming from nature, had to develop a psychological “self” to enable abstraction — which is what caused the first selfishness to ever be seen in nature (and in existence). You are jumping in from late interpretations of people who came much later (Plato and others) and using their understanding as evidence while failing to account for the mechanisms enabling the development. The idea of one god did not originate from Zarathustra nor Zoroastrianism, but had existed as the reality of nature and existence as one being — and that we all were this being. Differentiation, abstraction, cognition, reasoning hadn’t been developed and divided yet; so our perception was whole and complete. This explains why we all worshipped the light, the sun, the stars, and the fire, as we viewed god as being the light in nature and in ourselves. Asha literally means bones, which is where this fire is felt through the sensations of your body: giving us even the saying “in the bones”. Asha means bones in the protoafroasiatic group before becoming esh “fire” in the Semitic and later on “man” or simply the pronoun I in the Indo-European group. Without understanding language development, we can’t really understand our own history - as our languages and words are far older than any written text and interpretation. I love that you are opening the mind of the people though, but unfortunately truth is not prevailing.

### **@Tlilancalqui**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=Ugx2gwt9_WLmgGWyG0V4AaABAg)

He should point out a few things, or he doens't know,  Zarathustra did not invent Zoroastrianism, Zoroastrianism is the Persian wing of the Indo-Aryan cultural beliefs, Hinduism is the Indian wing, these beliefs were from the Black Sea area, and spread south to India, and West to Northern Europe and South to North Africa. 

The Jewish culture is also strongly influenced by this same culture, you can trace that through the bronze age where the northern Anatolian (Hurrian/Aryan) beliefs were as far as  Northern Egypt with the  Hyksos kings. After 1200 BC the Hurrian empire collapses, but the beliefs don't vanish, however the Assyrians, eventually go on a rampage to re-manufacture it, until they are eventually taken over by Darius, a Zoroastrian King. 

Oh and I should mention that the Minoan culture in Crete was also Indo-Aryan, and Greek thought is also heavily influenced by the same root. So no Zarathustra did not invent Zoroastrianism, it's far older, he just developed the Persian version.

### **@danielcohen4183**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgzZCO_IT3Dk4f8JiJV4AaABAg)

I think the use of the Monad as a general explanatory model oversimplifies important differences between religious traditions. While it may be useful as an abstract framework, it seems to flatten distinct approaches to the divine into a single idea of unity.

For example, in the Jewish perspective, there is a clear and fundamental distinction between human beings and God. The goal is not to become divine or merge with a higher essence, but to live morally, improve oneself, and serve God while maintaining that separation.

Because of that, presenting the Monad as a kind of neutral or foundational starting point can be misleading. It is not just a neutral tool, but a specific philosophical interpretation that does not fully represent all traditions.

I would argue that starting from the internal logic of each tradition, rather than reducing them to a single abstract model, provides a more accurate and respectful understanding.

> **@danielcohen4183**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dELEdxh0ceg&lc=UgzZCO_IT3Dk4f8JiJV4AaABAg.AUxmFuGgX80AUxmmS-SUiz)  
>
> Additionally, I want to state explicitly that from my perspective, Kabbalistic interpretations should not be presented as representative of Judaism. Many, including myself, do not accept Kabbalah as an authentic or authoritative part of the tradition.   
>   
> In the Hebrew Bible, some of the most severe consequences and national tragedies are explicitly linked to idolatry, and both the written text and the Oral Torah repeatedly command a strict separation from any form of it. For that reason, I find it deeply problematic that Kabbalistic systems incorporate elements and conceptual structures that, in my view, blur that boundary and resemble forms of idolatry within the system itself.  
>   
> Because of this, using Kabbalistic ideas as a bridge between Judaism and abstract philosophical models—such as the Monad—can be misleading, as it presents a version of Judaism that not all adherents recognize or accept.
